### Assistant (2026-05-01T12:53:20)

Stage 13 is structurally similar to Stage 12 but the decisions are different — Stage 12 was about **policy** (thresholds, gates), Stage 13 is about **presentation** (what the operator sees and acts on). Six question shape, in order of consequence:

---

### Q1 — Manifest medium and storage

**Decision**: where does the manifest *live*, and in what format?

| Option | Looks like | Pros | Cons |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Markdown file artifact** (per-process) | `artifacts/manifests/{process_id}/manifest.md` | Version-control friendly, human-readable, editable | Hard to filter/query, drifts from DB |
| **DB row only** (`manifests` table or extend `pipeline_assessments`) | structured columns | Queryable, single source | Not human-readable; needs UI to display |
| **Hybrid: markdown artifact + DB pointer** (recommended, mirrors Stage 11's run-manifest pattern) | `manifests` table with `manifest_path`, `manifest_hash`, `manifest_state`; the file lives in the artifact store | Best of both; aligns with existing artifact-store conventions | Two writes per generation |

**Sub-questions you should answer**:
- Does a `manifests` table exist already? (I think not — would need a migration. Easy to verify.)
- One manifest per process forever (with row_version on update), or per-`stage_run_id` history (append-only)?

**My recommended default**: hybrid — DB row + markdown artifact, one row per process with `row_version` bump on regeneration (mirrors Stage 11's `landed_cost_estimates` pattern, not history-per-run).

---

### Q2 — Manifest content scope (the big one)

**Decision**: what goes in a v1 manifest? Lean or comprehensive?

There are 6 content sections to choose from. Given today's 7 processes, here's what each section would look like for the **DGU process** as a worked example:

| Section | Lean v1 | Comprehensive v1 | DGU example content |
|---|---|---|---|
| **A. Header** | always | always | "Glass DGU procurement" + buyer + deadline + contract value + source (ProContract) |
| **B. Decision summary** | always | always | "1 candidate: 0 PASS, 1 REVIEW, 0 REJECT. Pipeline state: net_qualified. Review reason: quantity_basis_unaligned" |
| **C. Per-candidate detail** | only non-REJECT | every candidate | "DGU candidate: REVIEW. Reasons: [quantity_basis_unaligned, awaiting_rfq_quote, confidence_pricing_input_below_floor, confidence_revenue_below_floor]. Selected quote: alibaba-search 516p (CN). Landed cost band: 27,422 / 27,422 / 27,422p." |
| **D. Operator input requests** (actionable) | optional | always | "To unblock this candidate: (1) provide order quantity in m², (2) get RFQ quote, (3) confirm 5260p alibaba quote is realistic" |
| **E. Evidence trail** (links/IDs) | minimal (just IDs) | full (HMRC commodity code, freight quote URL, source documents) | `landed_cost_estimate_id=landed-pc-...`, HMRC HS-10=7008002000, SeaRates URL, ProContract source notice URL |
| **F. Coverage metrics** | basic (priced %) | full (priced/landed/net + value-weighted + unresolved lots) | priced_coverage=5000bps, landed_cost_coverage=5000bps, net_qualified_coverage=0bps, unresolved_mandatory_lots=1 |

**Sub-questions**:
- For un-priced processes (4 of 7 today), do you want a manifest that says "we found this opportunity but couldn't price it; here are the candidates the operator might want to manually evaluate" (your earlier ask), or a placeholder?
- Should the manifest include a "what changed since last regeneration" diff section?

**My recommended default**: **comprehensive A–F** because the manifest is the operator's primary decision surface; lean v1 risks under-informing. But split sections into collapsible/expandable in the UI (Stage 14 concern).

---

### Q3 — Process-level recommendation: pure facts vs suggested action

**Decision**: does Stage 13 emit a "we recommend X" verdict at the process level, or just present the Stage 12 facts?

| Option | What appears in the manifest |
|---|---|
| **Pure facts** | "1 candidate REVIEW. Reasons: [...]. Coverage: 50%. — operator decides what to do" |
| **Suggested action** with reasoning | "Suggested: CLARIFY (provide order quantity for the DGU candidate, then re-run). Reasoning: net_qualified_coverage=0 and the only blocker is quantity-basis." |
| **Stoplight only** (simplest) | 🟢 PURSUE / 🟡 CLARIFY / 🔴 PASS — one indicator, no detailed reasoning |

If you pick suggested action: how does Stage 13 derive it? Concrete rule sketch:
- ANY candidate PASS AND coverage ≥ 5000 → **PURSUE**
- ANY candidate `re_evaluation_eligible` (RFQ/quantity/no-pricing-signal) → **CLARIFY**
- All candidates REJECT OR coverage = 0 with no `re_evaluation_eligible` → **PASS**
- Otherwise → **REVIEW** (operator look)

**My recommended default**: **suggested action with reasoning + stoplight badge**. The operator gets a quick glance + a "why" they can interrogate. Pure facts is too thin; stoplight alone hides the reasoning.

---

### Q4 — Generation gating (always vs threshold)

**Decision**: does every Stage 12 process get a manifest, or only those above a coverage threshold?

| Option | Effect on today's 7 processes |
|---|---|
| **Always generate** (recommended for v1) | All 7 processes get a manifest. The 4 un-priced ones get a "no priced candidates yet — here's what we found and what you might want to manually evaluate" manifest |
| **Threshold-gated** (e.g., `priced_coverage_bps >= 5000`) | Only 3 of 7 processes get a manifest (the priced ones); 4 un-priced disappear from operator view |
| **Two-tier**: always generate stub; only "full" manifest above threshold | Stub for 4 un-priced; full for 3 priced. Operator sees all but knows which are sketchy |

This directly addresses your earlier "operator must see contracts even when we can't price them" concern.

**My recommended default**: **always generate**. Two-tier is overengineered for v1; stub-only un-priced manifests still surface the opportunity. Aligns with your stated intent.

---

### Q5 — Skill role: deterministic templater vs LLM judgment

**Decision**: what does the `manifest-generation` skill actually *do*?

The existing SKILL.md says "deterministic, process-scoped opportunity manifest synthesized from local pipeline data only" — strong hint it's a templater, not an LLM-judgment skill. But "deterministic" + skill + Claude CLI is unusual. Three readings:

| Option | What the skill does | Where the work lives |
|---|---|---|
| **Pure daemon templater** | Skill is a placeholder/no-op | Daemon assembles inputs + renders markdown via `format!` macros or a Rust template engine |
| **Skill renders markdown** (deterministic, agent-as-formatter) | LLM takes structured JSON input → emits structured markdown output to a contract | Daemon assembles + persists; skill formats |
| **Skill writes the recommendation reasoning** (Q3 "why" text) | LLM judges the structured Stage 12 evidence and writes natural-language explanation of suggested action | Daemon does facts; skill does the natural-language part |

**My recommended default**: option 2 (**skill renders markdown** following a strict result schema). Reason: the skill already exists with `headless_contract` mode and `result.schema.json`. Daemon assembles a structured input bundle, skill produces the markdown manifest as its result contract. Daemon validates against the schema, persists the artifact, writes the DB row.

**Important sub-question**: can option 2 be done deterministically with an LLM? Probably yes if the prompt is tight enough — but Stage 11 hardening showed prompts drift over time. Worth knowing this risk now.

---

### Q6 — Operator clarification scaffolding (Stage 13 ↔ Stage 14 contract)

**Decision**: does the manifest pre-format what the operator needs to provide?

This is the bridge between Stage 13 (read-only) and Stage 14 (operator writes). The cleaner Stage 13 makes the "what do I need to provide?" question, the easier Stage 14's UX becomes.

| Option | Manifest content |
|---|---|
| **No scaffolding** | Manifest just lists reasons (`quantity_basis_undetermined`); Stage 14 has to translate codes into operator-facing prompts |
| **Per-reason prompt** (recommended) | Each `re_evaluation_eligible` reason maps to a structured prompt: e.g. `quantity_basis_undetermined` → `{prompt: "Provide framework annual usage", unit: "kg", default_value: null}` |
| **Free-form text + structured prompts** | Both — the markdown reads naturally, AND there's structured JSON in frontmatter that Stage 14 binds to UI inputs |

**My recommended default**: **per-reason prompt as structured JSON in the manifest's frontmatter**, with the markdown body referencing it humanely. This commits Stage 13 to a small contract: every `re_evaluation_eligible` row in `candidate_net_margin_assessments` produces exactly one structured prompt object in the manifest.

---

### What I need from you

Pick one of each:
- **Q1**: lean / hybrid / DB-only
- **Q2**: lean v1 / comprehensive / something between (and: stub-for-unpriced y/n)
- **Q3**: pure facts / suggested action / stoplight only
- **Q4**: always-generate / threshold-gated / two-tier
- **Q5**: pure daemon / skill renders / skill writes reasoning
- **Q6**: no scaffolding / per-reason prompt / both

Plus any pushback on the recommended defaults — they're load-bearing assumptions, not just suggestions. Once I have your answers I'll do the codebase pre-flight (verify the `manifests` table situation, the skill input/output schema, the existing dispatch wiring) and write the handoff.

## Two things you should be aware of

### 1. Architectural deviation: the skill is no longer invoked

Read this carefully — it's the most important thing in the manifest's "Issues Found":

> Manifest contract adapters stalled during live Stage 13 attempts -> external Claude/Codex contract sessions did not complete promptly for the large manifest payload -> **added a daemon-local manifest contract renderer for Stage 13** and still validate the rendered payload through the skill result schema, typed deserialization, pass-through drift checks, frontmatter drift checks, and reasoning anchoring.

The agent **fell back from your Q5 hybrid choice** (skill renders markdown + LLM writes reasoning) to **Option 1** (pure daemon templater). The `manifest-generation` skill is currently NOT being invoked — the daemon renders the markdown AND writes the reasoning paragraph deterministically.

Practical implications:
- The natural-language `suggested_action_reasoning` text is now templated by the daemon, not LLM-creative. It's still "anchored to evidence" by construction (the daemon weaves in candidate names + reason codes), but it won't have the prose quality you'd get from Claude.
- The **drift detection** I designed (§2.7) is technically still running, but it's now comparing the daemon's bundle against the daemon's renderer output — both produced from the same source, so drift is impossible by construction. T.19/T.20/T.21 are still meaningful regression tests (they prove the validation logic itself works), but they're not catching real LLM drift.
- The skill files (`SKILL.md`, `result.schema.json`, `canary.inputs.jsonl`, `golden.assertions.yaml`) are still updated for the new contract, so when skill invocation is re-enabled later it should work.

This is a pragmatic fallback — skill timeouts on a 5-candidate-process payload were apparently blocking. **It's a reasonable trade-off, but it deviates from the design choice you signed off on.** If you want LLM-creative reasoning prose later, you'd need to investigate why Claude/Codex sessions stalled on this skill (large `job_parameters`? prompt complexity?) and either tune the skill or extend the timeout.

explain the skill fallback questions to me

---

### Assistant (2026-05-01T16:15:34)

Sure. Here's what happened and what's actually on the table.

## What was supposed to happen (your Q5 answer)

The Stage 13 design split work between daemon and skill:

| Concern | Owner |
|---|---|
| Compute deterministic stoplight, suggested_action label, clarification_prompts | **Daemon** (no LLM) |
| Render the markdown body (sections A–F) | **Skill** (Claude/Codex via headless contract) |
| Write the 1–2 sentence `suggested_action_reasoning` paragraph | **Skill** (the only LLM-creative output) |
| Validate skill output (schema + drift detection) | **Daemon** |
| Persist artifact + DB row | **Daemon** |

The point of putting the LLM in the loop was: deterministic facts everywhere except natural-language prose, where the LLM adds value (anchoring reasoning to specific candidate names + reason codes in a way templated text struggles with).

## What actually happened

The skill stalled. The manifest's "Issues Found" says:

> external Claude/Codex contract sessions did not complete promptly for the large manifest payload → added a daemon-local manifest contract renderer for Stage 13

So the agent built a **deterministic Rust renderer** that produces both the markdown AND the reasoning paragraph templated from the bundle. The `manifest-generation` skill exists, has the updated schema and prompt, but is **never actually invoked**. Every byte of the 7 manifests was written by Rust code.

## Why it probably stalled (educated guess)

I don't have logs from the failed attempts, but likely causes:

1. **Payload size**. A process with 5 candidates + landed-cost detail + coverage + clarification_prompts is multi-KB JSON. The skill prompt template has to fit that into context AND emit a multi-KB markdown response. Total prompt+response could push 20–50KB. Headless Claude CLI sessions can be slow on payloads that size.
2. **Latency × N processes**. Even at 30s per skill call, 7 processes × 30s = 3.5 minutes wall time if invoked serially. The Stage 11 timeout ceiling was 300s and Stage 11 still hit it occasionally. Stage 13 may have been hitting similar timeouts.
3. **Headless contract mode is brittle**. Stage 11's hardening rounds documented multiple failure modes around stream-json output buffering, protocol parser races, captcha intercepts, etc. Stage 13 inherits the same plumbing.

## What we lose vs gain

**Lost (vs the LLM design):**
- **Reasoning prose quality.** The daemon's templated reasoning will read like *"Stoplight is clarify because 5 candidates need operator input. Provide RFQ quotes for 2 candidates and order quantities for 3 candidates."* An LLM would write something more like *"This carpet framework has two priced lots awaiting buyer-quantity clarification before a margin floor can be established; the three remaining candidates need manual pricing or RFQ outreach. Recommend pursuing once at least the RFQ side returns."* Both convey the same facts; the LLM version reads more like an analyst's note.
- **Drift detection becomes tautological.** I designed §2.7 / T.19–T.21 to catch the LLM drifting from the bundle. With no LLM, there's nothing to drift — the daemon is comparing its own output to its own bundle. The validation logic still works (regression-safe if someone re-enables the skill later), but it's not catching real-world drift today.
- **Future flexibility.** Manifests that need richer narrative (cross-process summaries, "what's interesting this week", explanatory comparisons) are harder if the skill path is dormant.

**Gained:**
- **Speed.** Deterministic rendering is microseconds vs seconds-to-minutes per process.
- **Reliability.** No skill stalls, no agent-CLI flakiness, no model availability concerns.
- **Cost.** Zero inference spend per manifest run.
- **Determinism.** Same inputs always produce the same bytes — testable, diff-stable, hash-stable.
- **Operator UX.** A 1-second `--ingest manifest` run beats a 4-minute one.

For an L1–L3 MVP whose primary job is "show the operator what they need to decide", the templated reasoning is operationally sufficient. The LLM prose was a quality-of-life upgrade, not a correctness requirement.

## The decisions in front of you

| Option | Description | Effort | Cost | Quality |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **A. Accept the daemon-only fallback as v1** | Ship as-is. Templated reasoning is fine for MVP. Re-evaluate when L4-L6 lands or when richer manifests become a need. | 0 | $0 | Good enough |
| **B. Investigate the stall and re-enable full skill invocation** | Capture failed-run logs, identify whether it's payload size / model latency / CLI bug. Fix root cause; re-enable. Risk: rabbit hole. | Medium-high | $$ per manifest run | Best |
| **C. "Reasoning-only" hybrid** — skill JUST writes the paragraph | Daemon renders the entire markdown except the `suggested_action_reasoning` field. Skill is invoked with a tiny prompt: "Given this bundle, write 1–2 sentence reasoning." Payload becomes much smaller, stall risk drops. Daemon still owns 99% of the work. | Medium | $ per manifest run | Better |
| **D. Async / parallel skill calls** | Invoke all 7 skill calls concurrently instead of serially. Wall time drops from ~210s to ~30s. Doesn't help if individual calls stall; helps if it's queueing latency. | Medium | $$ | Same as B |
| **E. Smaller / faster model for this skill** | Drop to Haiku-class for rendering. SKILL.md already specifies `model_profile: structured_extract_low_cost` — could pin even lower. Faster but less prose quality. | Low | $ | OK |
| **F. Async/offline LLM enrichment** | Stage 13 ships fast (daemon-rendered). A background job re-renders the reasoning prose later when the LLM has time. Operator sees templated prose first, gets LLM upgrade later. | High (new orchestration) | $$ | Best, but complex |

## My recommendation

**Pick A or C.**

- **A (accept the fallback)** if you're focused on shipping Stage 14 and beyond. Templated reasoning is operationally fine; the architectural deviation is documented; you can revisit when the operator says "I wish the prose was richer."
- **C (reasoning-only hybrid)** if you want to preserve the spirit of your Q5 answer — the LLM does the one thing it's actually good at (writing prose) and nothing else. This is the smallest re-introduction of the skill path and most likely to work without stall issues, since the prompt becomes "given these facts, write 1–2 sentences" rather than "given these facts, render this entire markdown document."

I'd avoid B and F as a v1 — too much investigation/orchestration for a quality-of-life feature.

The question for you is essentially: **does the LLM prose matter enough today to spend time on it, or do we ship Stage 13 as-is and revisit later?** Either answer is defensible. Just want you to be making the call deliberately, not by default.

## Turn 1080

### User (2026-05-01T16:18:27)

Where are the costs from for option C? from morph llm im guessing?

---

### Assistant (2026-05-01T16:18:59)

Good question and worth correcting — it's **Anthropic API**, not Morph.

## Where the costs come from

The `manifest-generation` skill's frontmatter declares:

```yaml
agent_cli: claude
model_profile: structured_extract_low_cost
```

So when the daemon invokes the skill via `execute_skill_session`, it spawns a headless `claude` CLI session, which calls the Anthropic API. The cost is **Anthropic inference cost** (input tokens + output tokens for the agent's reasoning, tool calls, and output writing).

Morph LLM is integrated into this project, but for **code editing** (`mcp__morph-mcp__edit_file`) and **code search** (`mcp__morph-mcp__codebase_search`) — i.e., agent-driven development tooling. Pipeline skills like manifest-generation, HMRC customs, SeaRates, etc. all run via Claude CLI and bill against Anthropic.

## Concrete cost shape for Option C

If we take "reasoning-only hybrid" — skill is invoked with a tiny prompt and outputs only 1–2 sentences:

| Cost component | Approx |
|---|---|
| Input tokens | ~1–2k (the structured bundle compressed to facts the LLM needs to reason about) |
| Output tokens | ~50–150 (1–2 sentences) |
| Model | `structured_extract_low_cost` → probably maps to a Haiku-class model |
| Per-invocation | ~$0.001–$0.005 with Haiku 4.5; ~$0.01–$0.03 with Sonnet 4.6 |
| Per manifest run (today, 7 processes) | ~$0.01–$0.20 |
| Per busy week (~100 manifest re-renders) | ~$0.10–$3.00 |

Negligible at MVP scale. Cost only becomes meaningful if you fan out to thousands of processes per day.

## Why Option B (full markdown render) was more expensive

If the skill rendered the entire markdown body:

| Cost component | Approx |
|---|---|
| Input tokens | ~3–8k (full bundle) |
| Output tokens | ~2–5k (the multi-KB markdown body) |
| Per-invocation | ~$0.05–$0.30 with Sonnet (or ~$0.01–$0.05 with Haiku) |
| Per manifest run | ~$0.35–$2.10 |

Still small in absolute terms — but **20–40× the cost of Option C** because the LLM has to emit ~3000 markdown tokens instead of ~100 reasoning tokens. And large outputs are also where the stalls happened.

## Practical implication

Option C makes the LLM do the thing LLMs are uniquely good at (write a paragraph that anchors structured facts in natural language) while leaving the boring high-volume rendering to deterministic Rust. That's the right cost-quality split — daemon does the cheap deterministic work, LLM does the small expensive prose.

Cost-wise, Option C is essentially free at MVP scale. The reason to consider it isn't budget — it's whether you care about prose quality for the operator's experience.

Im confused, we aren't using the api. We are running everything locally and leveraging my existing subscription.

### Assistant (2026-05-01T16:20:19)

You're right — I had that wrong. Let me re-frame.

## What's actually being used

The `claude` CLI in headless contract mode authenticates against your existing subscription (whatever account you're logged in with). It's not billing through the Anthropic API key, so there's no per-token charge.

## The real "cost" is non-monetary

With subscription-backed inference, the trade-off shape changes:

| "Cost" axis | Option B (full markdown) | Option C (reasoning-only) |
|---|---|---|
| **Wall-clock latency per process** | ~30–60s (large output) | ~5–15s (small output) |
| **Subscription quota usage** | Heavier — large output tokens count against your limits | Lighter — small output, less quota burn |
| **Rate-limit risk** | High at scale (7 manifests serially → minutes; concurrent → could trip throttling) | Low (small calls, less likely to throttle) |
| **Stall risk** | High (this is what bit Stage 13) | Low (small payload = less time in the brittle CLI/contract pipeline) |
| **Determinism** | Same as C | Same as B |
| **Operator wait time on `--ingest manifest`** | 4–8 min for 7 processes | <1 min for 7 processes |
| **Dollars** | $0 (subscription) | $0 (subscription) |

So the real reason Option C is attractive isn't budget — it's that **a small focused prompt is much less likely to stall**, finishes faster, and burns less of your subscription quota. The subscription is the cheap part; latency and reliability are what bit Stage 13.

## Implications for the decision

Since dollar cost isn't a constraint, the question simplifies:

- **Option A (accept the daemon-only fallback)** — zero LLM time, zero stall risk, deterministic. But no LLM prose ever.
- **Option C (reasoning-only)** — small per-process LLM call, low stall risk, gets you the prose quality back for ~5–15s extra wall time per process.
- **Option B (full markdown)** — what stalled. Probably not worth re-attempting at the original payload size.

If your subscription tier comfortably handles ~7 small calls per `--ingest manifest` invocation (and probably 100s/day at production volumes), Option C becomes a clean win for prose quality at no real cost beyond a minute or two of wall time.

The decision really is just: **do you want LLM-quality reasoning prose enough to add ~10s/process latency to manifest generation?** If yes → C. If no → A. Dollars don't enter the picture.

Apologies for the API-cost framing earlier — that was wrong for your setup.

### Assistant (2026-05-01T16:27:32)

Handoff written to `prompts/stage-13-skill-invocation-restoration-handoff.md` (~770 lines).

## Structure

**Mission**: turn off the daemon-local fallback, route Stage 13 through actual skill invocation, restore the LLM-creative reasoning prose. Goal state = `skill_session_ids` non-NULL on all 7 manifests.

**The Loop**: investigate → patch → validate → re-run → fix until every R passes with skill mode enabled.

## Six key design choices

1. **Feature flag with three modes** (`STAGE13_RENDERER_MODE`): `skill` / `daemon` / `auto`. Default stays `daemon` during investigation so production doesn't break; flips to `skill` only after Option B is GREEN. `auto` mode is the safety net — try skill, fall back to daemon on error.

2. **Investigation-first methodology (§3)**: don't speculate. Reproduce on the smallest process (`proc-pc-218726bd-...`, 1 candidate), capture session artifacts with timestamps (T0=spawn, T1=first event, T2=result.json, T3=exit), categorize the stall into one of 6 categories, THEN apply the matching fix.

3. **Six ordered fix candidates (§4)**:
   - **F.1** raise timeout (smallest fix, no code change beyond a const)
   - **F.2** concurrent skill invocation (`buffer_unordered(3)`)
   - **F.3** verify the model is actually Haiku-class, not accidentally Sonnet
   - **F.4** trim the bundle (saves ~10–30% input tokens)
   - **F.5** borrow Stage 11's protocol-parser hardening (result-recovery after timeout/parse-error)
   - **F.6** CLI/auth diagnostics
   - **F.7** explicit "stop and consult" if F.1–F.6 don't fix it (don't silently degrade to Option C)

4. **Hard-stop criteria (D5)**: if the smallest process stalls past 900s even with F.1+F.2 applied, write a RED manifest and stop. Don't ship "auto mode that always falls back" — that's just Option A with extra latency dressed up as Option B.

5. **9 new unit tests T.22–T.30**:
   - T.22–T.27: feature-flag behavior (default, override, fallback, frontmatter persistence)
   - T.28: deterministic fields stay byte-stable across LLM renders (stoplight, prompts don't drift even though prose does)
   - T.29: drift detection rejects diverging stoplight (this is now **genuinely meaningful** — was tautological in daemon-only mode)
   - T.30: reasoning anchoring — generic boilerplate like "this opportunity needs review" is rejected; the LLM must reference specific candidate names or reason codes

6. **Determinism scope split (D4)**: stoplight/clarification_prompts/manifest_basis_hash stay bit-stable; markdown body and reasoning paragraph may differ run-to-run because LLM is non-deterministic. R.16 (re-runnability) is split accordingly: deterministic fields must reproduce; LLM prose may vary but must still pass anchoring.

## 16 acceptance criteria

Highlights:
- **R.1**: `confidence_breakdown.skill_session_ids.manifest_generation` non-NULL on all 7 rows (the THE definition of success — proves skill ran)
- **R.5**: reasoning anchoring — each `suggested_action_reasoning` references at least one candidate name or reason_code
- **R.9/R.10**: Stage 11/12 columns + DGU row + 17 candidate verdicts unchanged (Stage 13 work doesn't bleed into earlier stages)
- **R.13**: `auto` mode falls back correctly when skill errors — kill-test included
- **R.14**: total wall time <5min for `--ingest manifest`
- **R.15**: default mode flipped to `Skill` after work completes
- **R.16**: 129 binary unit tests pass (120 prior + 9 new)

## What's NOT in scope

- Stage 14 (operator decision)
- Wiring `--ingest manifest` into `run_deterministic_pipeline`
- Anthropic API integration (sticks with subscription-backed CLI)
- Removing the daemon-local renderer (it stays as the rollback path)

## Validation queries cover

- skill_session_ids and `skill_invocation_outcome` per manifest
- agent_session_logs rows for manifest-generation skill
- Stoplight/prompt counts unchanged from current daemon-rendered state (proves pass-through fields stay deterministic)
- Stage 11/12 columns invariant
- Idempotency (re-run produces 0 changes)
- Auto-fallback behavior under forced 1s timeout

Want me to do a fresh-eyes audit on this handoff before you send it, or is it good to ship?

## Pattern observation

The biggest catch this round (5 of 7 bugs) was **column-name assumptions in `agent_session_logs`**. I wrote the validation queries from memory using "common-sense" names (`skill_id`, `outcome_class`, `session_id`) — every single one was wrong. The actual schema uses `skill_path`, `status`, `pty_session_id`. This is the same failure mode as round 3 caught in Stage 13 (`raw_price_gbp_pence` vs `unit_price_gbp_pence`).

**Lesson for me**: when I'm writing SQL against a table I haven't directly queried recently, I should always `.schema` it first, never write from memory.

The const-with-env-var bug is the most embarrassing — that's a basic Rust language error I should have caught when re-reading my own example.

- 696 lines
- 16 R criteria, 9 T tests, 5 D decisions, 7 F fix candidates

- All Rust patterns now compile-able (or have explicit caveats about needed refactor)
- `Stage13RenderOutput` shape defined

This round caught more bugs than I expected — 7 vs my round-5 estimate of "diminishing returns." The schema-name failures were genuine factual errors, not stylistic. Without this audit:
- Agent would have hit a Rust compile error on F.1
- Every validation query would have returned 0 rows or errored
- R.1 would have been impossible to verify (column doesn't exist)
- F.2 would have failed to compile from the start

If you want a 7th audit, I'd target it at: actually running the validation queries against the live DB to confirm they execute (not just syntax-check). Or you can ship — the schema-name fixes are the load-bearing ones, and they're done.

## Honest answers

1. **Re-read with fresh eyes**: yes, with intentional skepticism. Round 5 lulled me into thinking we were near done; round 6 proved we weren't.
2. **Followed skill instructions**: not invoking a slash-skill — manual handoff work.
3. **Studied codebase**: this round caught me — I'd been writing SQL from memory rather than schema-querying. Now corrected.
4. **Optimal and clear**: substantially better than draft 1. The schema fixes alone make the validation path actually executable.
5. **Perfect context**: very close, but I keep proving "perfect" is asymptotic. Each round catches what I missed in the prior. Ship when you've reached your tolerance for marginal improvement.

**The Option B work is much smaller than my prior rounds implied.** The current Stage 13 architecture is well-decomposed:

```
load_manifest_skill_bundle → render_manifest_skill_output_from_bundle → 
serde_json::to_value → validate_manifest_skill_output → persist_manifest_generation_output
```

Option B's actual job is to **add ONE new helper** (`execute_manifest_skill_session` returning `Value`) and a **mode dispatch** that picks between the new helper and the existing daemon-local renderer. Everything else (validation, drift detection, persistence, the `ManifestSkillBundle` type, the `ManifestSkillOutput` type) is reused unchanged.

Estimated work delta:
- Earlier rounds implied: extract renderer, define new types, build validation, plumb persistence — multi-day work
- Round 7 reality: add one helper + one mode dispatch + add `Cli: Clone` derive (or skip concurrency) + build `extract_frontmatter` (~20 lines) — half-day to one day

# Stage 13 Skill Invocation Restoration (Option B) Validation Manifest

> Generated: 2026-05-01T18:08:30Z

## Summary
Option B succeeded. The original stall was a combination of model/profile latency and the child process continuing after `result.json` had already been written; the first diagnostic used the CLI default Opus-class model and spent extra time producing cleanup/run-manifest work after the contract was complete. The fix restores real `manifest-generation` skill invocation, pins Stage 13 to Haiku by default, terminates the child after a stable `result.json`, recovers valid result contracts after SIGTERM exit 143, validates the skill output through the existing drift suite, and invokes up to 3 skill workers concurrently. The final full skill-mode run rendered all 7 manifests in 199.45s, and all persisted manifest frontmatter now has `renderer_mode: skill`.

## Investigation Findings
- Stall category: model-latency plus post-result overrun/protocol lifecycle cleanup. CLI/auth was healthy; payload size was not the primary blocker.
- Smallest-process T0/T1/T2/T3: T0 `2026-05-01T17:12:45.426372+00:00`; T1 `2026-05-01T17:12:47.958975+00:00`; T2 `2026-05-01T17:13:36.219569+00:00`; T3 `2026-05-01T17:13:37.740852+00:00`.

- Total wall time for full `--ingest manifest` (skill mode): 199.45s with `STAGE13_RENDERER_MODE=skill`, `STAGE13_SKILL_TIMEOUT_SECONDS=900`, default concurrency 3.
- Logs path: `/tmp/stage13_diag_smallest.log`, `/tmp/stage13_diag_smallest_after_fixes.log`, `/tmp/stage13_diag_largest_after_frontmatter_fix.log`, `/tmp/stage13_full_skill_run.log`, `/tmp/stage13_r12_rerun.log`, `/tmp/stage13_r13_auto.log`.

## The model question — properly explained

Two distinct things, easy to conflate:

### Build-time vs runtime

| Layer | Tool | Model | Subscription used |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Build** (writing the Stage 13 code) | Codex (OpenAI CLI) | GPT-class via OpenAI | OpenAI account (ChatGPT/API) |
| **Runtime** (the manifest skill being invoked by Stage 13 at `--ingest manifest`) | `claude` CLI per `agent_cli: claude` in SKILL.md | Haiku (pinned via `STAGE13_CLAUDE_MODEL=haiku` default) | Claude subscription |

You used Codex to BUILD. That used your OpenAI side, not Claude. The daemon at runtime calls `claude` for the manifest skill — that's where your Claude subscription gets used.

### Why Haiku, not Opus 4.7

Look at the actual model resolution code (`crates/daemon/src/main.rs`):

```rust
fn claude_model_for_skill_profile(skill: &LoadedSkill) -> Option<String> {
    if skill.metadata.skill_id == "manifest-generation" {
        return Some(env::var("STAGE13_CLAUDE_MODEL").unwrap_or("haiku".to_owned()));
    }
    match skill.metadata.model_profile.as_str() {
        "structured_extract_low_cost" => Some("haiku".to_owned()),
        _ => None,
    }
}
```

For the manifest skill: explicitly pinned to Haiku, overridable via env. This was a deliberate choice in Option B — the agent's investigation showed:
- **Opus** (CLI default if you're logged into Claude Max): too slow for R.14's <5min budget. Stage 11 hardening also hit Opus latency on contract sessions.
- **Sonnet**: reliable but still too slow for 7 processes within 5 minutes.
- **Haiku**: 199.45s for all 7 processes ✓.

**Is Haiku "the necessary model for the task"?** Yes, genuinely. The manifest skill is doing structured rendering + 1-2 sentence reasoning paragraph. Haiku is sized for exactly this: lots of structure echo + small natural-language output. Opus's strengths (multi-step reasoning, nuanced judgment) don't add value here — the daemon already computed the verdict, stoplight, and prompts deterministically. The skill is just a prose-shaper.

### What about other skills?

Per-skill model resolution:

| Skill | model_profile in SKILL.md | Code resolution | Effective model at runtime |
|---|---|---|---|
| `manifest-generation` | `structured_extract_low_cost` | explicit override → `STAGE13_CLAUDE_MODEL=haiku` | **Haiku** |
| `hmrc-customs-classification` | `browse_stable_low_cost` | falls through `match` → returns `None` | **CLI default** (whatever `claude` decides) |
| `searates-freight-estimate` | `browse_stable_low_cost` | same — None | **CLI default** |
| `shipment-sizing` | `browse_stable_low_cost` | same — None | **CLI default** |
| `simple-fx-lookup` | `browse_stable_low_cost` | same — None | **CLI default** |
| `procurement-ingestion/fts`, `contracts-finder`, etc. | varies | varies | **CLI default** |

**Real implication:** the `match` arm in `claude_model_for_skill_profile` only handles `structured_extract_low_cost`. Every other profile returns `None`, which means no `--model` flag is passed to the CLI, and the CLI uses whatever model your subscription's logged-in session defaults to. If you're on Claude Max, that's likely Sonnet 4.6 or Opus 4.7 depending on your settings.

This is not necessarily wrong — Stage 11's customs/freight/FX skills genuinely need more reasoning than rendering, so Sonnet/Opus is appropriate. But it means **the daemon doesn't pin Stage 11 to a specific model**; it inherits whatever the CLI is configured for. If you want predictable quality + cost, you'd want explicit pins for those skills too.

### What you should consider (not blocking, but worth knowing)

1. **Manifest on Haiku is correct** — leaves your Opus quota for skills that actually need it. Don't change.
2. **Stage 11 skills are unpinned** — they ride the CLI default. If you specifically want them on Opus 4.7 (to maximize quality of customs classification, etc.), add explicit pins. If you want them on Sonnet (to save quota), pin to Sonnet. Either is reasonable.
3. **For BUILDING with Codex**: if you'd rather use your Claude Max subscription for code-writing too, switch from Codex to `claude` CLI (or Claude Code) when executing handoffs. Same handoff document; different agent runtime. This consolidates your spend onto one subscription.

The TL;DR for your "leveraging subscriptions and using the best models" framing:
- ✅ Manifest skill = Haiku, intentionally and correctly.
- ⚠️ Stage 11 skills = whatever CLI default. If you want to control this, add explicit pins.
- ⚠️ Code-writing was Codex (OpenAI). If you're paying for Claude Max, use Claude Code for the agent that builds handoffs to consolidate spend.

## Turn 1146

### User (2026-05-01T19:03:55)

Can you present me the decisions like we did for previous stages for stage 14?

---

### Assistant (2026-05-01T19:05:55)

Stage 14 is the operator's daily surface — they read a manifest, decide what to do, and the system records it. Unlike Stages 12/13 (mostly policy + tech), Stage 14 is mostly **operator UX + a small write path**, plus one unique-to-the-current-data wrinkle: every process today is `stoplight=clarify`, so the operator's first job is mostly answering clarification prompts, not approving/rejecting.

Six question shape, ordered by consequence:

---

### Q1 — Disposition vocabulary

**Decision**: what can the operator choose at decision time?

| Option | Vocabulary | Pros | Cons |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Simple 3-state** | `approve` / `reject` / `watch` | Minimal cognitive load; matches L2 strategy spec | No way to express "needs more info first" or "team escalation" |
| **4-state** | + `hold` (defer; revisit later) | Captures "I'll come back to it" | Drift toward unbounded queues |
| **5-state** | + `escalate` (route to teammate / management) | Useful when shared authority lands in L4 | Today there's only one operator, so escalate is no-op |
| **Multi-axis** | (decision: pursue/pass/defer) + (reasoning_code: pricing/timing/risk/...) + (next_action: bid_now/await_rfq/...) | Rich audit trail | Schema complexity; over-engineered for single-operator MVP |

**Today's reality**: every process is `stoplight=clarify`, so the operator's first action is rarely a final approve/reject — it's answering clarification prompts (Q2). The disposition vocabulary still matters for the eventual decision after clarifications come back, but it's not the primary flow on day 1.

**My recommended default**: **4-state — `approve / reject / watch / hold`**. Approve = pursue this opportunity; reject = drop it; watch = monitor for changes (re-render manifest if Stage 12 re-runs and notify if stoplight changes); hold = "I'll come back later, don't archive". L2 strategy mentions `desired_disposition` as the field name on `pending_operator_intents` — fits 4-state cleanly. Skip `escalate` until L4 introduces multi-operator.

**What you need to tell me**: 3-state, 4-state, or other.

---

### Q2 — Clarification prompt handling (unique to today's data)

**Decision**: when an operator opens a manifest with `clarification_prompts` (today: 18 prompts across 7 processes, 16 candidates), what do they do?

| Option | Flow |
|---|---|
| **A. Answer inline → trigger re-run → decide on updated manifest** | Operator types order qty / RFQ price into a UI form. System writes to `operator_quantity_overrides` (new table, L1-L3 scope) or extends `pending_operator_intents` with override fields. Daemon re-runs Stages 11→12→13 for that candidate. Operator returns to a fresh manifest. |
| **B. Answer separately → re-run notifies operator → operator decides** | Operator answers prompts in a "Clarifications" panel. The daemon re-runs in the background. Operator is notified when the manifest is fresh. Two-step flow. |
| **C. Don't capture clarifications in Stage 14** | Operator handles RFQs/quantity-discovery out-of-band (call buyer, ask team), then comes back and decides on the existing manifest using their out-of-band knowledge. No new tables, no re-run flow. |

**Today's data is the primary driver** for this decision. With every process needing clarification before it can auto-pass, options A or B are operationally meaningful. Option C is simpler but means the operator can't actually act on most processes via Stage 14 today — they'd have to go elsewhere first.

**My recommended default**: **Option A (inline)**. Reasons:
- Today's data needs clarification before disposition makes sense; without it, the operator decision UX is hollow
- Inline form binds directly to the structured `clarification_prompts` from the Stage 13 frontmatter (already byte-equivalent across markdown + DB column)
- Re-run is cheap (`--ingest landed-cost`, `--ingest net-gate`, `--ingest manifest` chain) and we already proved it idempotent
- Adds one new table (`operator_input_overrides`) but stays in L1-L3 scope

**Required new schema** if Option A: a small `operator_input_overrides` table keyed by `(process_id, candidate_id, reason_code)` with payload-shape matching the `clarification_prompts` schema. Stage 11/12 read it before computing.

**Sub-question**: should overrides be **persistent** (survive across re-runs forever) or **versioned** (tied to a specific `manifest_basis_hash`, become stale if upstream changes)?
- Persistent = simpler; operator's input remembered until they un-set it
- Versioned = more correct but harder UX (operator may be asked the same question again after upstream changes)

I'd suggest **persistent until explicitly changed**, with a freshness signal in the desktop UI (e.g., "this override was provided 3 days ago, before the latest re-pricing").

**What you need to tell me**: A / B / C, and persistent-vs-versioned for overrides.

---

### Q3 — Re-decision policy

**Decision**: can an operator change a previous decision? Under what conditions?

| Option | Behavior |
|---|---|
| **Immutable** | Once decided, the intent is locked. Mistakes require an explicit "void + new intent" action with a justification. |
| **Mutable until executed** | Operator can change disposition any time before bid_workflow (Stage 15) actually fires. After execution, immutable. |
| **Mutable always with audit chain** | Every change creates a new `pending_operator_intents` row with `superseded_by_intent_id` pointing to the previous one. Full history; latest wins. |
| **Auto-invalidate on re-evaluation** | When Stage 13's `manifest_basis_hash` changes (because Stage 12 produced new verdicts), any existing intent against the old hash is auto-marked stale; operator must re-confirm against the new manifest. |

L2 strategy doc has `superseded_by_intent_id` as a field, suggesting the architecture was designed for "mutable with audit chain". L0 also has the `reviewed_basis_hash` and `reviewed_manifest_hash` fields, suggesting "auto-invalidate on re-evaluation" is also expected.

**My recommended default**: **mutable with audit chain + soft auto-invalidate**. Concretely:
- Operator can change a decision anytime → creates new intent row, old one marked `superseded_by_intent_id`
- When Stage 13 re-runs and produces a new manifest_basis_hash, the existing intent's `reviewed_basis_hash` no longer matches → intent goes into `status='stale'` (still queryable, but the desktop UI prompts for re-confirm before bid_workflow can fire)
- Bid workflow (Stage 15, future) requires `status='active'` AND matching basis hash — this prevents acting on a stale decision

This combines the audit-chain benefit (full history) with the freshness guarantee (can't bid on outdated information).

**What you need to tell me**: which option, and whether bid_workflow should be blocked on stale intents (vs warn).

---

### Q4 — Evidence presentation at decision time

**Decision**: what does the operator see when they open a process for decision?

| Option | UI shape |
|---|---|
| **Manifest-only** | Render the Stage 13 markdown body. Operator reads, scrolls, decides. |
| **Manifest + Quick-Decide panel** | Markdown body + sticky panel with stoplight badge, suggested_action, disposition buttons, basis_hash for audit |
| **Manifest + drill-downs** | + ability to click into per-candidate detail, raw landed_cost, search_attempts evidence; everything is read-only but inspectable |
| **Manifest + Quick-Decide + drill-downs + clarification form** | Full kitchen sink: Q2 inline form, drill-downs, structured panel, plus the markdown |

**My recommended default**: **Manifest + Quick-Decide panel + drill-downs + clarification form** — the full kitchen sink. Reasoning:
- The operator is making real-money-affecting decisions; they should see every piece of evidence
- Stage 13's manifest is already comprehensive (Q2 from the Stage 13 design); the desktop UI just needs to render it richly
- Drill-downs are read-only; no risk to data integrity
- Clarification form is required if Q2 = Option A

This is the most ambitious UI option, but the data is all already in place. The work is presentation.

**What you need to tell me**: one of the four, or a custom mix.

---

### Q5 — Ship shape: CLI-first vs Desktop UI vs both

**Decision**: what's the minimum viable Stage 14?

| Option | Concretely |
|---|---|
| **CLI-first** | `cargo run -- --capture-decision <process_id> --disposition approve --basis-hash <hash> --reasoning "$TEXT"`. Persists `pending_operator_intents`. No UI. Operator reads the markdown manifest with their preferred tool (cat, less, browser); decision capture is one CLI invocation. |
| **Desktop UI** (Tauri, full operator surface) | Polished operator panel with manifest rendering, decision buttons, clarification form, drill-downs. Significant frontend work. |
| **Both, CLI first then desktop** | Ship CLI in week 1 (proves the data path), then build desktop on top. Desktop reuses the same `pending_operator_intents` write path. |

**Today's reality**: there's only one operator (the user). They can plausibly use a CLI for daily ops if it's well-shaped. Desktop UI is more polish than necessity for the immediate user.

**My recommended default**: **Both, CLI first**. Specifically:
- Phase 1 (this Stage 14 handoff): CLI capture path + `pending_operator_intents` write + Q2 inline-clarification CLI variant + re-run trigger. Desktop UI is a stub.
- Phase 2 (a follow-up Stage 14b handoff or absorbed into L2 polish): Tauri Desktop UI on top. The CLI keeps working as a power-user fallback.

This gets the data path proven without blocking on UI. L2 already has Tauri scaffolding; Stage 14b becomes a UI build, not a data-architecture build.

**What you need to tell me**: CLI-first / Desktop / both. If both, do you want the CLI handoff first (Phase 1), or both in one larger handoff?

---

### Q6 — Audit metadata required vs optional

**Decision**: what does the operator MUST capture alongside the decision?

L2 strategy doc lists these fields on `pending_operator_intents`: `process_id`, `desired_disposition`, `reviewed_basis_hash`, `reviewed_manifest_hash`, `reviewed_at_stage_run_id`, `reviewed_at`, `base_remote_revision`, `idempotency_key`, `status`, `superseded_by_intent_id`, `queued_at`. Required by the schema. Plus there's a question about additional metadata.

| Option | What's required beyond the schema fields |
|---|---|
| **Minimal** | Only the schema-required fields (auto-populated from manifest + timestamp) |
| **Recommended** | + free-form `reasoning_text` (operator types why they decided) |
| **Strict** | + structured `decision_tags` (closed enum: pricing-too-high / timing-issue / capacity-mismatch / regulatory-risk / not-our-domain / pursuing-via-other-channel / ...) |
| **Strict + reasoning** | Both: structured tag (one-of) AND optional free-form reasoning |

**My recommended default**: **Recommended (free-form reasoning_text, optional)** for v1. Reasoning:
- Free-form is fast for the operator (no pick lists)
- "Optional" means the operator can decide quickly when reasons are obvious
- Structured tags are valuable for analytics later but slow daily ops; can add in L4-L6 when there's enough data to make tags useful
- The `reviewed_basis_hash` + `reviewed_manifest_hash` give us "what they saw" anyway; reasoning_text adds "why they decided"

**Sub-question**: should the operator be **required to acknowledge they read the manifest** (e.g., "Yes, I've read it" checkbox) before disposition is allowed? Forces deliberateness; adds friction. I'd say **no** — the very act of opening the decision UI is the implicit acknowledgement; making them click extra is friction-for-friction-sake.

**What you need to tell me**: minimal / recommended / strict.

---

### What I need from you

Pick one for each:

1. **Q1 disposition vocabulary**: 3-state / 4-state / 5-state / multi-axis
2. **Q2 clarification flow**: A inline / B separate-then-notify / C out-of-band; plus persistent-vs-versioned overrides
3. **Q3 re-decision policy**: immutable / mutable-until-executed / mutable-with-audit-chain / auto-invalidate
4. **Q4 evidence presentation**: manifest-only / +Quick-Decide / +drill-downs / kitchen-sink
5. **Q5 ship shape**: CLI-first / Desktop-only / both (and order)
6. **Q6 audit metadata**: minimal / recommended / strict

Plus push back on any defaults that don't match how you want operators to actually work day-to-day. Stage 14 is the operator's daily-driver UX, so this one's worth thinking through carefully — easier to design correctly upfront than refactor after operators build muscle memory around the wrong workflow.

Once I have your answers I'll do the codebase pre-flight (verify `pending_operator_intents` schema columns, check for any existing decision-capture stubs, look at the Tauri app's current state) and write the handoff.

## Architecture summary

**One new migration** (`0009_operator_input_overrides.sql`) → bumps epoch to 6 → renames `pdf_t_schema_activation_009_default_schema_epoch_is_5` → `_is_6` (same gotcha as Stages 12 + 13).

**Five new CLI subcommands** as top-level Cli flags (not `--ingest` arms):
- `--capture-decision --process-id --disposition --idempotency-key [--reasoning]`
- `--capture-clarification --process-id --candidate-id --reason-code --payload [--note]`
- `--withdraw-clarification --process-id --candidate-id --reason-code`
- `--withdraw-decision --idempotency-key`
- `--list-decisions [--process-id] [--list-status-filter]`

**Override injection in 3 places** (small, localized changes):
- Stage 11's `infer_order_quantity_basis` (line 3449) — check overrides first, fall through to existing inference
- Stage 10's revenue resolver — when `awaiting_rfq_quote` override exists, treat `rfq_required` as `expected_value` with operator-supplied revenue
- Stage 10's pricing-assessment synthesis — when `no_pricing_signal` + `manual_price` variant override, synthesize a pricing assessment from operator data

**Stale detection** runs inside `run_manifest_generation_live_loop` post-write (one SQL UPDATE in the same transaction). Transitions any `queued` intent whose `reviewed_basis_hash != new_manifest.manifest_basis_hash` to `status='stale'`.

## Loop pattern + tests

- **18 new unit tests** (T.1–T.18) covering every override variant, idempotency, audit chain, stale detection, FK integrity. Target: 129 → 147 binary unit tests.
- **End-to-end demo (§3)** — explicit step-by-step bash sequence (clarify DGU → re-run pipeline → manifest changes → decide → re-decide → stale → withdraw → list). Must be captured to `/tmp/stage14_demo.log` and referenced from the validation manifest.
- **16 acceptance criteria** (R.1–R.16) with inline SQL validation queries.
- **9 failure-mode entries** mapping symptoms to remediations.
- **Constraints** lock scope: don't touch desktop, Stage 11/12/13 stay structurally unchanged, 3-state disposition non-negotiable, idempotency mandatory.

## Today's data context baked in

The §3 demo uses the DGU process (`proc-pc-7046e8ec-...`) as the canonical happy-path scenario:
1. DGU's `quantity_basis_unaligned` clarification at qty=100 m² → re-run → DGU manifest refreshes (10000 m² supplier-capacity replaced with 100 m² operator-supplied)
2. Decision = `approve` with reasoning
3. Re-decide as `watch` → first decision auto-superseded
4. Update qty to 250 m² → re-run → 002 transitions to `stale`
5. Withdraw → `--list-decisions --list-status-filter queued` returns empty

| # | Bug | Severity | Why it slipped through |

Plus field names: `RfqRequired { fallback_qty, basis_unit }` not `{ qty, unit }` | Build break — agent would write `match` arms against non-existent variants | I described from memory instead of grepping |
| 2 | **Override-injection via signature change was too invasive** — modifying `infer_order_quantity_basis(row)` to take `(&Connection, row)` ripples through 17 callers (mostly tests). Replaced with cleaner row-extension design: add `operator_override: Option<...>` field to `LandedCostAssessmentRow`, populate at load time, keep helper pure | Architectural over-reach — would have been a sprawling change for the agent | I designed the API in the abstract without checking caller count |
| 3 | **Stage 10 entry point was vague** — "compute_gross_margin_gate (or whatever the rfq-required revenue path is)". Actual function: `allocate_gross_margin_revenue` at `main.rs:17687`. Now cited 6 times with line number | Agent has to grep around to find it; minor speed hit | I'd been describing what the function should do without checking what it's called |
| 4 | **`no_pricing_signal` synthesis was hand-wavy and architecturally complex** — Stage 10's flow is built around `supplier_quotes`; injecting a synthetic pricing assessment is non-trivial. Simplified to **RECORDED ONLY in Phase 1** (the override row is captured but doesn't auto-flow through the pipeline). Auto-synthesis deferred to a follow-up bead | Scope reduction — keeps Phase 1 focused; the operator can still decide based on knowledge of the manual price even if the manifest doesn't auto-update | I'd spec'd a feature without checking implementation cost |
| 5 | **Stale detection placement was wrong** — I put it at the `run_manifest_generation_live_loop` level, but Stage 13 has 3 render modes (skill / daemon / auto-fallback). One mode could forget to fire. Moved INSIDE `persist_manifest_generation_output` (the shared persistence helper) so all 3 paths automatically trigger | Real correctness issue — would have been a silent reliability gap | I'd added the spec at the level I was reading code, not where it shared the most callers |
| 6 | **§3 demo had 4 duplicate `--capture-*` lines** in bash (copy-paste errors from earlier draft) — `cargo run -p daemon -- --capture-clarification \` followed by `--capture-clarification proc-pc-...` | Demo wouldn't run as written | Sloppy bash composition |
| 7 | **`OrderQuantityBasis` doesn't auto-derive `Serialize`** — the `confidence_breakdown.order_quantity_basis` JSON shape `{kind, qty, source, unit}` is built manually somewhere (the actual variants don't have `source` field). Adding the new variant requires finding that builder and adding a case | Subtle — the agent would discover this when their JSON didn't match the expected `kind="from_operator_override"` | I assumed `#[derive(Serialize)]` without grepping |
| 8 | **No warning about Rust enum-variant compile cascade** — adding `FromOperatorOverride` to an enum makes every existing `match` arm a compile error until updated. With 17 call sites, this matters. Added explicit warning | Helps the agent expect the cascade, not get surprised | Standard Rust gotcha; I omitted the warning |

# Stage 14 Operator Decision (Phase 1 — CLI) Validation Manifest

> Generated: 2026-05-01T20:36Z
> Status: GREEN

## Summary

Stage 14 Phase 1 ships the operator-decision CLI capture path end-to-end. The §3 demo walked the DGU process from clarification capture (`quantity_basis_unaligned`, qty=100 m²) through pipeline re-run, manifest re-render with a fresh `manifest_basis_hash`, decision capture (`approve` then `watch`), idempotency no-op, audit-chain supersede, stale detection (after a second clarification at qty=250 produced a third manifest basis), withdrawal, and queued-list filter. The override injection in Stage 11 changed the DGU's `landed_cost_estimates.confidence_breakdown.order_quantity_basis` from `rfq_required (qty=10)` to `from_operator_override (qty=100 → qty=250)` across two pipeline reruns. The `manifest_basis_hash` cycled `b2cbfc… → c51aba… → fb75ba…`, and `pending_operator_intents` accumulated three statuses (superseded, stale, withdrawn) across two intents — exactly as designed. Stage 12 picked up 17 candidates as `verdict='review'`, all 7 manifests stayed `manifest_ready/clarify`, and zero FK orphans on the new override / intent tables.

## Build Summary

- Migration: `migrations/0009_operator_input_overrides.sql` (new file; bare DDL, no `BEGIN;`/`COMMIT;`).
- Migration registered: `crates/daemon/src/db/sqlite.rs` `MIGRATIONS` array, epoch 6, plus `verify_l3_schema` now also asserts `operator_input_overrides` exists.
- Schema activation tests:
  - Renamed/replaced epoch test: `pdf_t_schema_activation_009_default_schema_epoch_is_5` → `_is_6` (asserts `DEFAULT_SCHEMA_EPOCH == 6` AND `user_version == 6`).
  - New: `opd_t_schema_activation_009_upgrade_path_applies_0009_cleanly` (replays migrations 0001-0008, opens StateDb, asserts override table + columns + both indexes + bumped user_version).
- New CLI subcommands on `Cli`: `--capture-decision`, `--disposition`, `--idempotency-key`, `--reasoning`, `--capture-clarification`, `--candidate-id`, `--reason-code`, `--payload`, `--note`, `--withdraw-clarification`, `--withdraw-decision`, `--list-decisions`, `--process-id`, `--list-status-filter`. Dispatched in `dispatch_operator_action(...)`, called from `main()` BEFORE the `--ingest` match.
- Override-injection points:
  - **Stage 11**: `LandedCostAssessmentRow.operator_override: Option<OperatorQuantityOverride>` populated by `load_landed_cost_assessments` via `read_active_quantity_override`. `infer_order_quantity_basis` short-circuits to `OrderQuantityBasis::FromOperatorOverride { qty, basis_unit }`. New variant added to `kind/source/supplier_cost_basis/quantity/unit/to_json/screening_uncertainty_warning` impls. `compute_supplier_cost_basis` short-circuits when the override variant is active. `JSON kind` emitted as `"from_operator_override"`.
  - **Stage 11 freshness**: the loader's WHERE clause now includes `OR EXISTS (... operator_input_overrides ... AND oio.updated_at > lce.updated_at)` so the live-loop reprocesses landed-cost rows when an override is freshly captured or withdrawn.
  - **Stage 10 RFQ**: `allocate_gross_margin_revenue` now reads `read_active_rfq_override` first; on hit, returns `RevenueAllocation { revenue_basis_type: "expected_value", revenue_pence = unit_price × qty, confidence_bps: 8000 }` where qty comes from a quantity-override or RFQ `moq` fallback.
  - **`no_pricing_signal`**: recorded only — no auto-synthesis. Confirmed by T.12.
- Stale detection: `mark_intents_stale_on_basis_drift(tx, process_id, new_manifest_basis_hash)` called inside `persist_manifest_generation_output` (the shared persistence helper used by all 3 Stage 13 render modes), inside the same `with_write_transaction`.

## Issues Found & Fixed

1. **Move-after-borrow in `capture_operator_decision`**: the `with_write_transaction` closure captured `snapshot` by move, leaving the post-transaction `json!({...})` unable to read `snapshot.decision_basis_hash` / `artifact_hash`. Fix: clone both fields into `_for_query` locals before the closure.
2. **Loader staleness**: the original `load_landed_cost_assessments` WHERE clause only refreshed lce rows on `quote_row_version` drift or non-empty blockers, so a fresh override would not trigger reprocessing. Fix: added `OR EXISTS (... operator_input_overrides ... AND oio.updated_at > lce.updated_at)`. Verified by step 3a/9 — both pipeline reruns picked up the DGU candidate (attempted=1).
3. **Clippy `too_many_lines` / `struct_field_names` / `redundant_clone` / `useless_conversion` / `similar_names`**: all addressed inline (added `#[allow]` for the four legitimately long handler functions; renamed `cli_a1/a2/b1` to `cli_alpha/beta/gamma`; dropped redundant clones; collapsed `Ok(...?)`).
4. **Migration epoch test rename**: anticipated by handoff §0; renamed `..._is_5` → `..._is_6` to match the new `DEFAULT_SCHEMA_EPOCH = 6`. Same gotcha as Stages 12 and 13.

## Two issues to address before Stage 15 / L4

### 1. Stage 14 work is uncommitted (recurring pattern)

Same problem as Stage 13: the implementation isn't in git.

```
M crates/daemon/src/db/sqlite.rs                  (epoch=6, MIGRATIONS entry)
M crates/daemon/src/main.rs                        (~500+ lines of Stage 14 code, T.1-T.18)
M crates/daemon/tests/schema_activation.rs         (renamed epoch test + new opd_t test)
```

Plus the migration file `migrations/0009_operator_input_overrides.sql` is likely untracked too (let me confirm with you — gitignore probably doesn't catch it but it wasn't in the modified list).

If you'd like, I can run the same hygiene pass we did after Stage 13 (commit the modified files + the migration file, push to origin, verify clean state). Same caveats apply: a `git restore` would lose Stage 14's schema epoch + tests + entire CLI surface.

### 2. The Stage 14 build's environmental gap (informational, not a defect)

The DGU `landed_cost_estimates` row has `blockers=[CDP_CONNECT_FAILED, SEARATES_NO_RESULT]` because the validation env didn't run CDP. This means the second pipeline re-run (after qty=250 clarification) couldn't actually re-fetch fresh SeaRates freight numbers — the row's freight columns may be stale relative to the new quantity. In a production run with CDP up, qty=250 would re-trigger a SeaRates lookup with the larger size and produce updated freight.

Stage 14's override injection IS correctly working (qty 100→250 captured, basis_kind correct, manifest re-rendered). The downstream Stage 11 freight wasn't fully refreshed because of the env limitation. **Worth noting for when you're ready to do a full real-data exercise of the operator workflow**: bring up CDP first.

## Stage 15 is L6 — correctly blocked

Per the strategy docs, Stage 15 (bid workflow / request pipeline automation) is squarely L6 territory. It depends on:
- L4 (Supabase sync + remote `operator_decision_heads` with CAS) for cross-machine decision authority
- L5 (broker-grade customs/freight) for confident enough estimates to actually bid against
- L6 (request pipeline automation) for the eSourcing portal automation itself (Proactis is named as the initial target)

None of L4/L5/L6 are built yet. Building Stage 15 now would mean either:
- Building it on L1-L3 foundations only (incomplete; would break when L4 lands and changes the operator-decision authority model)
- Building L4-L6 first (multi-month strategic work)

## What's actually still buildable / QA-able within L1-L3 scope

Things you CAN do without crossing the L4-L6 boundary:

| Item | Layer | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| **Stage 14 Phase 2 (Tauri Desktop UI)** | L2 polish | The CLI plumbing is in place; Phase 2 wraps it in a UI. `apps/desktop/` already has scaffolding |
| **Stage 11 hardening — order-quantity inference** | L1 polish | Right now non-RFQ candidates can have the "supplier capacity vs order qty" quirk we documented. A small bead could improve `infer_order_quantity_basis` for `expected_value` candidates |
| **Stage 11 hardening — CDP captcha resilience** | L1 polish | The deterministic-fallback policy exists; could add cached-evidence path or operator-prompted re-fetch |
| **Stage 13 follow-up — manual_price auto-synthesis** | L1 follow-up | The deferred bead from Stage 14: when operator captures `no_pricing_signal manual_price`, auto-synthesize a `candidate_pricing_assessments` row so it flows downstream and the operator can decide on a real margin |
| **Multi-source ingestion validation** | L1 | We have ProContract data; FTS + Contracts Finder skills exist but haven't been driven against fresh notices. Could prove the 3-source ingestion + relate-stage dedup actually works |
| **Real-data run breadth** | L1 polish | Today's data is 133 ProContract notices, 4 priced candidates. More variety (different HS families, different origins, non-GBP quotes) would exercise more of Stage 11's customs/freight/FX paths |
| **End-to-end operator workflow with multiple processes** | L1-L3 polish | Run `--ingest manifest` → operator clarifies → re-runs → decides on 5+ processes back-to-back to find UX friction |

## My recommendation for what to do next

Three credible paths:

1. **Stage 14 Phase 2 (Desktop UI)** — biggest user-facing improvement. Operator currently has to use CLI; Phase 2 makes Stage 14 actually pleasant. Builds on existing data plumbing; no new schema. Tauri scaffolding exists.

2. **Multi-source + multi-candidate real-data exercise** — drive FTS or Contracts Finder ingestion to get 50+ more candidates, exercise breadth of Stage 11's customs/freight/FX, then walk a few through the operator decision flow. Catches integration gaps the current narrow data doesn't surface.

3. **Strategic pivot to L4** — start L4 work (Supabase sync, web portal). Largest scope but unblocks Stage 15 eventually. Per memory, planmaxer iteration 003 already generated L4-L6 strategy artifacts in 2026-03; you could pick those up.

Personal lean: **path 1 first** (Stage 14 Phase 2 — small, finishes a half-built feature, gives the operator real value), then **path 2** (broader data exercise to find what L1-L3 doesn't handle well), then decide whether to invest in L4 or do another pass of L1-L3 hardening.

## Parallel-safety design

**No file overlap on the load-bearing work surfaces:**

| Handoff | main.rs primary lines | Other files |
|---|---|---|
| 1 (Phase 2 UI) | none-to-minimal | `apps/desktop/` — isolated dir |
| 2 (order qty) | ~3449, ~3804 | none |
| 3 (CDP captcha) | ~4070 | `skills/landed-cost/searates-freight-estimate/` |
| 4 (multi-source) | ~10486 (and Stage 3 area) | `skills/procurement-ingestion/{fts,contracts-finder}/` |

The four agents' main.rs sections are **non-overlapping** (3449 / 4070 / 10486 are 600–6000 lines apart). Skill directories are completely disjoint. The only mild risk is if any of them needs to add a `pub(crate)` exposure that lands at the bottom of main.rs — coordinate via `git pull --rebase` at merge time.

**Each handoff's §0 Pre-flight explicitly notes:**
- Use `git worktree add ../testproject1-<name> main` for clean isolation
- What main.rs sections / files are theirs
- What sections / files are explicitly OFF-LIMITS (other handoffs' territory)
- Merge to main with rebase at the end

**No migration epoch conflicts:**
- None of the four handoffs adds a new migration. Stage 14 Phase 2 is UI-only; the two Stage 11 hardenings are logic-only; multi-source ingestion drives existing tables.

## Recommended execution order (if you can't parallelize all 4)

If running fewer than 4 agents simultaneously, this ordering minimizes thrash:

1. **Multi-source ingestion** (#4) — first, because it expands the data surface that the other handoffs operate on. New FTS/Contracts Finder candidates give the Stage 11 hardening more variety to validate against. Doesn't conflict with anything else.
2. **Stage 11 hardening — order quantity** (#2) AND **Stage 11 hardening — CDP captcha** (#3) — these two are the most work, can run concurrently, both touch Stage 11 but in different functions.
3. **Stage 14 Phase 2 — Desktop UI** (#1) — last, because it benefits from having more processes to render. Most isolated work surface (`apps/desktop/`).

If running all 4 simultaneously: just use worktrees and rebase carefully. The conflict surface is small.

## Validation/fix loop in each

All four follow the same pattern (consistent with Stage 11 hardening / Stage 12 / Stage 13 / Stage 14 Phase 1 handoffs):
- **The Loop diagram** at the top
- **§0 Pre-flight** with verifiable commands
- **§1 Decisions** baked in
- **§2 Implementation order**
- **§3 Demo** (live walkthrough)
- **§4 Required tests** (specific T.* / R.* / FT.* / CDP.* / OQ.* / MS.* numbered)
- **§5 Acceptance criteria** (R.1–R.X)
- **§6 Validation queries** (live SQL)
- **§7 Failure-mode playbook** (symptom → cause → fix table)
- **§8 Constraints** (including parallel-safety notes)
- **§9 Mandatory deliverable** (validation manifest at `docs/pipeline/<name>-validation-manifest.md`)
- **§10 Reminders**

Each handoff's manifest target is named distinctly so they don't collide:
- `docs/pipeline/stage-14-phase-2-validation-manifest.md`
- `docs/pipeline/stage-11-hardening-order-quantity-validation-manifest.md`
- `docs/pipeline/stage-11-hardening-cdp-resilience-validation-manifest.md`
- `docs/pipeline/multi-source-ingestion-validation-manifest.md`

Want me to do a fresh-eyes audit on any of the four before you ship them out, or are they good to go as-is?

## Third-pass audit summary

This pass found 5 substantive bugs that the first two missed by going beyond surface-level checks into actual operational math and Rust signature reality:

**1. CDP retry math overshoots `LANDED_COST_SKILL_TIMEOUT_SECONDS`** (build-passing but runtime-broken):
- Original: 3 attempts × 90s + (30s + 90s + 180s) backoffs = **390-570s** vs 300s outer ceiling
- Fix: tightened to 3 × 60s + (15s + 45s) = 240s + 5s persistence = **245s ≤ 300s**
- Added explicit math table to the handoff with rule "if you change either, redo the math"

**2. CDP cached freight CANNOT WORK on first run** (demo would silently fail):
- Verified live DGU lce row has `freight_base_gbp_pence = 0`. Cache target doesn't exist.
- Scenario B as written would always fall through to Scenario C output, masking the cache-fallback codepath.
- Fix: added 3-option pre-condition guidance (run Scenario A first / seed synthetic prior row / skip and rely on CDP.6 unit test) plus updated expected output to acknowledge both branches.

**3. Stage 14 daemon helpers take `&Cli`, not typed args** (architectural blocker):
- `capture_operator_decision(state_db, cli, process_id)` reads disposition + idempotency_key from CLI fields. Tauri-side cannot invoke this without faking a Cli struct.
- This is precisely the bug behind the existing `capture_decision_impl` parallel write path in apps/desktop/.
- Fix: explicitly called out the required refactor (extract typed-arg version, have CLI handler adapt) before any Phase 2 IPC wiring proceeds.

**4. Multi-source false claim about pipeline cascade**: `--ingest manifest` runs ONLY `run_manifest_generation_live_loop` (verified main.rs:1262), NOT a Stages 2-13 cascade as the failure-mode playbook claimed. Fix: corrected playbook entry + added note in §3 demo about manual stage-by-stage walking + Stage 4-9 agent-orchestration boundary caveat.

**5. Wrong column name + existing-test acknowledgment**: `manifests.manifest_basis_hash` (not `basis_hash`); existing `infer_order_quantity_basis_*` tests at main.rs:22124-22186 may need updating after `BasisUndetermined` variant added; existing `stale_detection_*` tests at 23646/23677 must continue to pass after Phase 2 refactor.

## Cumulative across all 3 passes

**22 substantive bugs caught and fixed** across 4 handoffs:
- Pass 1 (12 bugs): line numbers, IPC name collision, wrong skill metadata, wrong baseline, FTS asymmetry
- Pass 2 (5 bugs): wrong test runner, wrong crate name, missing ManifestViewer awareness, missing ContractList isStale awareness, wrong canonical_ocid join
- Pass 3 (5 bugs): retry math overshoot, empty cache pre-condition, helper signature problem, false cascade claim, wrong column name + existing-test risk

The handoffs are now grounded in verified codebase claims, fit operational budgets, acknowledge architectural realities, and remain parallel-safe (each in its own worktree, non-overlapping main.rs regions: ~3821, ~4497, ~13786, apps/desktop/).

# Stage 14 Phase 2 — Validation manifest

**Branch:** `main`
**Date:** 2026-05-02
**Phase:** Stage 14 Phase 2 — Tauri desktop UI for operator decision

Phase 1 (committed at `91a1484`) shipped the CLI capture path. Phase 2 wraps the
same data-plumbing in a Tauri desktop UI so the operator works in a graphical
control panel instead of a terminal. Data plumbing is unchanged.

---

## §1 Scope delivered

### Daemon — new IPC handlers

`crates/daemon/src/ipc/handlers/clarifications.rs` (new module). Three new
typed-param IPC handlers wired through the existing `DaemonBridge` Unix socket:

| Wire method            | Handler fn                | Backed by                                              |
|------------------------|---------------------------|--------------------------------------------------------|
| `capture_clarification`| `capture_clarification`   | Same SQL as `capture_operator_clarification` (CLI)     |
| `withdraw_clarification`| `withdraw_clarification` | Same SQL as `withdraw_operator_clarification` (CLI)    |
| `withdraw_decision`    | `withdraw_decision`       | Same SQL as `withdraw_operator_decision` (CLI)         |

`crates/daemon/src/ipc/command_router.rs`: 3 new dispatch arms + import wiring +
`map_clarifications_error` mapper.

`crates/daemon/src/ipc/handlers/decisions.rs`: extended `CaptureDecisionParams`
with `decision_note: Option<String>` and persists it into
`pending_operator_intents.decision_note`. Phase 1 had silently dropped any
reasoning text the UI sent.

`crates/daemon/src/main.rs`: 8 surgical `pub(crate)` exposures so the new
handler module can call shared helpers without code duplication
(`OperatorOverrideReason`, `OperatorOverrideReason::parse`,
`OperatorOverrideReason::as_str`, `validate_operator_override_payload`,
`persist_stage`, `lookup_intent_by_idempotency_key`, `OperatorIntentRow`,
`read_latest_manifest_basis_hash`, `sha256_hex`,
`OPERATOR_DECISION_STAGE_NAME`, `DEFAULT_LOCAL_OPERATOR_ID`).

### Daemon command router — full method list

Phase 2 takes the published method count from **11 → 14**:

```
get_daemon_info, get_cron_status, get_source_health_status,
get_validation_status, get_resource_arbiter_status, restart_daemon,
trigger_ingestion, trigger_enrichment,
capture_decision, capture_clarification, withdraw_clarification, withdraw_decision,
resolve_error, retry_job, stop_session, send_session_input, resize_session,
rerun_from_error
```

### Tauri (desktop Rust) — new bridge surface

`apps/desktop/src-tauri/src/daemon_bridge.rs`:
- New params: `CaptureClarificationParams`, `WithdrawClarificationParams`,
  `WithdrawDecisionParams`. Existing `CaptureDecisionParams` extended with
  `decision_note`.
- New connection methods: `capture_clarification`, `withdraw_clarification`,
  `withdraw_decision`.
- `DaemonCommandMethod` enum extended from 11 → 14 variants.

`apps/desktop/src-tauri/src/query_service.rs`:
- New VMs: `ManifestInboxRow`, `DecisionHistoryEntry`, `ActiveOverrideEntry`.
- New `QueryService` methods: `list_manifests`, `get_decision_history`,
  `get_active_overrides`.
- Existing `ManifestVm` extended with `clarification_prompts_json`,
  `manifest_basis_hash`, `stoplight`, `suggested_action` so the detail page
  has everything the new operator panel needs.

`apps/desktop/src-tauri/src/main.rs`:
- 7 new `#[tauri::command]` functions: `list_manifests`, `get_decision_history`,
  `get_active_overrides`, `capture_clarification`, `withdraw_clarification`,
  `withdraw_decision`, `trigger_pipeline_rerun`.
- `trigger_pipeline_rerun` reuses `trigger_enrichment` under the hood; the
  React side polls `manifest_basis_hash` for completion.
- Existing `capture_decision` was reused (no new symbol); only its forwarder
  `bridge_capture_decision_params` was extended to pass `decision_note` through.

### React frontend — new screens

`apps/desktop/src/pages/ManifestInbox.tsx` (new): default route at `/inbox`.
List of all manifest-ready processes with stoplight badges (clarify=yellow,
pursue=green, pass=red, review=gray), suggested action, prompt count,
decision pill (with stale indicator), updated-at timestamp.

`apps/desktop/src/pages/OperatorDecisionPanel.tsx` (new): self-contained
section that handles all five Phase 2 surfaces:
1. **Quick-Decide panel** — three disposition buttons + reasoning textarea.
   Reasoning is sent to the daemon as `decisionNote` and persisted into
   `pending_operator_intents.decision_note`.
2. **Clarification form** — one fieldset per `clarification_prompts` entry
   from the manifest frontmatter. `prompt_type=number` → number input;
   `prompt_type=choice` → select; `prompt_type=object` → JSON textarea.
3. **Active overrides** — list of currently-applied operator overrides.
4. **Pipeline rerun** — button that invokes `trigger_pipeline_rerun`. The
   parent refetches the detail view via `onChanged()` and the manifest
   basis hash updates surface naturally.
5. **Decision history** — full audit chain (queued/superseded/stale/withdrawn)
   with status badges and a Withdraw button on the latest queued intent.

`apps/desktop/src/pages/ContractDetail.tsx`: extended with one new prop
(`onPanelChanged`) and slots `OperatorDecisionPanel` between the existing
decision controls and the notice/lots grid. Legacy decision controls remain
intact for backwards compatibility with `App.tsx`'s existing wiring.

`apps/desktop/src/App.tsx`: added the `/inbox` route, a "Manifest Inbox" nav
button, an `inbox` `RouteState` variant, and a `currentRoute` clause for it.
The `onPanelChanged` callback triggers a refetch of `get_process_detail`.

### Stale banner

`OperatorDecisionPanel` reads `latestDecision.isStale` (already supplied by
`get_process_detail`'s existing decision rollup) and renders the stale banner
inline above the Quick-Decide panel.
